But is this always optimal? Why place at maximum?

Because placing at maximum:
- Kills the monster at maximum position by explosion
- Pushes all monsters with position < maximum to position - r
- These pushed monsters might now be ≤ r, so they could die by traps if they get pushed to ≤ 0
- But in this case, we're pushing them to y - r. If y - r ≤ 0, they die from traps.

In the above strategy, when we push a monster from position y to y - r, if y - r ≤ 0, it dies from traps.

So the algorithm is:
1. Sort the monster positions
2. While there are monsters remaining:
   - Find the maximum position among current monsters
   - Place explosion at that position
   - Remove monsters at that exact position (they die by explosion)
   - For all other monsters at position y < max_position, push them to y - r
   - If y - r ≤ 0, they die by traps (remove them)
   - Otherwise, they survive and are added to the current positions list with new position y - r
3. Count the number of such shots

But we need to be careful about performance. The total n across queries is ≤ 10^5, but if we simulate each shot and update positions, and if there are many shots, it might be slow.

However, each monster can only be pushed a finite number of times (specifically, until their position becomes ≤ r, where they die by traps). Actually, no - when pushed, position becomes y - r, then if that is still > r, it might survive.

In fact, a monster at position y will be pushed to y - r, then possibly to y - 2r, etc., until it becomes ≤ r, at which point if y - k*r ≤ 0, it dies.

But the position decreases by r each time it's pushed. So after k pushes, it's at y - k*r.

It will eventually die when y - k*r ≤ 0, which happens after k ≥ ceil(y/r).

But the total sum of n over queries is only 10^5, and each monster can be pushed at most O(max_position/r) times. Since max_position ≤ 10^5 and r ≥ 1, this could be up to 10^5 per monster, which is too slow.

We need a more efficient approach.

After research in known problems, I recall that this is similar to a problem where the minimum number of shots is the number of distinct values in the array after we take floor((x_i - 1)/r) or something.

Another idea: after each shot, the positions of the surviving monsters are reduced by r for those to the left.

The process will terminate when all positions are <= r, and then one more shot will kill them all.

So if we let S be the set of initial positions, then after a series of shots, the positions become S' = { x - k*r | x in S, k>=1, x - k*r >0 }

And eventually all become <= r.

The number of shots is the number of times we need to apply the reduction until all are <= r.

But the reduction is not applied independently; it's applied at the maximum position.

However, in the end, every monster will be pushed a certain number of times, and the total number of shots is the maximum over all monsters of the number of times it is pushed, plus possibly one for the final shot when all are <= r.

In example 1: monster at 1: pushed at first shot (when explosion at 5) to 1-2 = -1, so pushed once. Monster at 3: pushed to 3-2=1, then in next shot when explosion at 1, it is not pushed (since 1 is not to the left of 1), so only once. Monster at 5: exploded once.

Then after, only monster at 1 (from 3), then shot at 1.

So for the monster that was at 3, it was pushed once, then in the next shot, when explosion at 1, it is at position 1, which is not < 1, so not pushed.

So the number of times a monster is pushed is not the same as the number of shots.

- Shot 1 at 5: monster at 1 (y=1<5) pushed to 1-2 = -1 (dies), monster at 3 (y=3<5) pushed to 3-2=1 (survives)
- Now positions: [1]
- Shot 2 at 1: monster at 1 dies

So the monster at 3 was pushed once, the monster at 1 was pushed once, the monster at 5 was exploded.

Algo for each query:

3. shots = 0
4. while positions is not empty:
   a. shots += 1
   b. c = positions[-1]  # current maximum
   c. new_positions = []
   d. for each position in positions:
        if position > c:  # should not happen since c is max
           new_position = position + r
           new_positions.append(new_position)
        elif position == c:
           # dies by explosion, skip
           continue
        else: # position < c
           new_position = position - r
           if new_position <= 0:
               # dies by trap, skip
               continue
           else:
               new_positions.append(new_position)
   e. positions = new_positions
5. return shots

We can use a while loop with a list.

Let me implement with a list and at each step, we use the list as sorted.

In Python, we can do:

```python
import sys

def solve():
    q = int(input())
    for _ in range(q):
        n, r = map(int, input().split())
        positions = list(map(int, input().split()))
        positions.sort()
        shots = 0
        while positions:
            shots += 1
            max_pos = positions[-1]
            new_positions = []
            for pos in positions:
                if pos == max_pos:
                    # dies by explosion
                    continue
                elif pos < max_pos:
                    new_pos = pos - r
                    if new_pos > 0:
                        new_positions.append(new_pos)
                # if pos > max_pos: should not happen
            positions = new_positions
        print(shots)

solve()
```

```python
import sys

def main():
    q = int(input())
    for _ in range(q):
        n, r = map(int, input().split())
        positions = list(map(int, input().split()))
        positions.sort()
        shots = 0
        while positions:
            shots += 1
            max_pos = positions[-1]
            new_positions = []
            for pos in positions:
                if pos == max_pos:
                    continue
                elif pos < max_pos:
                    new_pos = pos - r
                    if new_pos > 0:
                        new_positions.append(new_pos)
            positions = new_positions
        print(shots)

if __name__ == '__main__':
    main()
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Ivan plays an old action game called Heretic. He's stuck on one of the final levels of this game, so he needs some help with killing the monsters.

The main part of the level is a large corridor (so large and narrow that it can be represented as an infinite coordinate line). The corridor is divided into two parts; let's assume that the point $x = 0$ is where these parts meet.

The right part of the corridor is filled with $n$ monsters — for each monster, its initial coordinate $x_i$ is given (and since all monsters are in the right part, every $x_i$ is positive).

The left part of the corridor is filled with crusher traps. If some monster enters the left part of the corridor or the origin (so, its current coordinate becomes less than or equal to $0$), it gets instantly killed by a trap.

The main weapon Ivan uses to kill the monsters is the Phoenix Rod. It can launch a missile that explodes upon impact, obliterating every monster caught in the explosion and throwing all other monsters away from the epicenter. Formally, suppose that Ivan launches a missile so that it explodes in the point $c$. Then every monster is either killed by explosion or pushed away. Let some monster's current coordinate be $y$, then:

  if $c = y$, then the monster is killed;  if $y < c$, then the monster is pushed $r$ units to the left, so its current coordinate becomes $y - r$;  if $y > c$, then the monster is pushed $r$ units to the right, so its current coordinate becomes $y + r$. 

Ivan is going to kill the monsters as follows: choose some integer point $d$ and launch a missile into that point, then wait until it explodes and all the monsters which are pushed to the left part of the corridor are killed by crusher traps, then, if at least one monster is still alive, choose another integer point (probably the one that was already used) and launch a missile there, and so on.

What is the minimum number of missiles Ivan has to launch in order to kill all of the monsters? You may assume that every time Ivan fires the Phoenix Rod, he chooses the impact point optimally.

You have to answer $q$ independent queries.


-----Input-----

The first line contains one integer $q$ ($1 \le q \le 10^5$) — the number of queries.

The first line of each query contains two integers $n$ and $r$ ($1 \le n, r \le 10^5$) — the number of enemies and the distance that the enemies are thrown away from the epicenter of the explosion.

The second line of each query contains $n$ integers $x_i$ ($1 \le x_i \le 10^5$) — the initial positions of the monsters.

It is guaranteed that sum of all $n$ over all queries does not exceed $10^5$.


-----Output-----

For each query print one integer — the minimum number of shots from the Phoenix Rod required to kill all monsters.


-----Example-----
Input
2
3 2
1 3 5
4 1
5 2 3 5

Output
2
2



-----Note-----

In the first test case, Ivan acts as follows:   choose the point $3$, the first monster dies from a crusher trap at the point $-1$, the second monster dies from the explosion, the third monster is pushed to the point $7$;  choose the point $7$, the third monster dies from the explosion. 

In the second test case, Ivan acts as follows:   choose the point $5$, the first and fourth monsters die from the explosion, the second monster is pushed to the point $1$, the third monster is pushed to the point $2$;  choose the point $2$, the first monster dies from a crusher trap at the point $0$, the second monster dies from the explosion.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have monsters on a coordinate line with x > 0 (right part)
2. If a monster reaches x ≤ 0, it gets killed by traps
3. When we launch a missile at position c:
   - Monsters at position c are killed immediately
   - Monsters at position y < c are pushed left to y - r
   - Monsters at position y > c are pushed right to y + r
4. We want to find the minimum number of missiles to kill all monsters

## Key Insights

1. When we launch a missile at position c, monsters to the left of c (y < c) move left by r units, which might push them to x ≤ 0 (killed by traps)
2. Monsters to the right of c move right by r units, making them even farther from origin
3. Monsters at exactly c are killed instantly
4. The optimal strategy is to target positions where we can both kill monsters directly and push some monsters into the trap zone (x ≤ 0)

## Strategy Analysis

Let me think about when we should launch missiles:
- If we launch at a position c, monsters at y < c get pushed to y - r
- A monster at position y will be pushed to x ≤ 0 when y - r ≤ 0, i.e., y ≤ r
- So monsters initially at positions ≤ r can be killed by being pushed into the trap zone when we launch at a position ≥ y
- Monsters at positions > r will always move away when pushed left

Actually, let me reconsider: if a monster is at position y and we launch at position c where y < c, it moves to y - r. This will be killed if y - r ≤ 0, i.e., y ≤ r.

So the key insight is: any monster initially at position ≤ r can potentially be killed by being pushed left into the trap zone when we launch at a position c ≥ y.

For monsters at position > r, they will either be killed directly if we launch at exactly their position, or they will be pushed further right (making them harder to kill).

Wait, this seems inefficient. Let me reconsider the example:

Example 1: n=3, r=2, positions [1,3,5]
- Launch at 3: 
  - Monster at 1: 1 < 3, so moves to 1-2 = -1 ≤ 0 → killed by trap
  - Monster at 3: at position 3, so killed by explosion
  - Monster at 5: 5 > 3, so moves to 5+2 = 7
- Launch at 7:
  - Monster at 7: killed by explosion
- Total: 2 shots

Example 2: n=4, r=1, positions [5,2,3,5]
- Launch at 5:
  - Monster at 5: killed by explosion
  - Monster at 2: 2 < 5, so moves to 2-1 = 1
  - Monster at 3: 3 < 5, so moves to 3-1 = 2
  - Monster at 5: killed by explosion
- Now positions are [1,2] 
- Launch at 2:
  - Monster at 1: 1 < 2, so moves to 1-1 = 0 ≤ 0 → killed by trap
  - Monster at 2: at position 2, so killed by explosion
- Total: 2 shots

## Key Observations

1. Monsters initially at positions ≤ r can be killed by a single missile if we position it at a suitable point ≥ their position
2. For monsters at positions > r, they can only be killed either directly (by launching exactly at their position) or by being pushed further away (which doesn't help)
3. So for monsters initially at positions > r, we need to either:
   - Kill them directly with a missile at exactly their position
   - Or use a strategy that eventually pushes them to be killed later

Wait, actually when we launch at c for a monster at position y > c, it moves to y + r, which is even farther right. This seems counterproductive.

But looking at the examples, in the second example, monster at position 2 gets pushed to position 1 when launched at 5, and then at position 1 when launched at 2, it gets killed by trap.

So the key insight is: we can use a "chain reaction" where we launch at a large position to push monsters to the left, and then launch at a smaller position to kill the ones that are now at positions ≤ r.

## Strategy

The key insight is that we can kill monsters at positions ≤ r by pushing them left when we launch at a position c ≥ their position.

For monsters at positions > r, when we launch at c < x, they move to x + r, which increases their distance from origin. When we launch at c > x, they move to x - r, which might bring them into the trap zone.

So a monster at position x can be:
- Killed by explosion if we launch at exactly x
- Killed by trap if we launch at c such that x - r ≤ 0, i.e., x ≤ r (and c ≥ x)
- Or moved to x + r when c < x

But if x > r, then x - r > 0, so even if we launch at c ≥ x, it only moves to x - r > 0, which is still positive.

So for a monster at position x > r:
- It can only be killed by explosion (by launching at exactly x)
- Or by being pushed to x + r, then later being killed when we launch at x + r

Wait, if we push it to x + r, then when we launch at x + r, it gets killed by explosion.

So a monster at position x > r can be killed by:
1. Launching at x (direct explosion)
2. Launching at some c < x (pushing it to x + r), then launching at x + r (explosion)

But launching at c < x pushes it to x + r > x > r, so it just moves farther right - this doesn't help.

If we launch at c < x, the monster at x (where x > c) gets pushed to x + r.

So it moves from x to x + r, which is farther away from origin.

This seems counterproductive.

But wait, in the first example, monster at position 5 > r=2, we launch at 3 < 5, so it goes to 5 + 2 = 7, then we launch at 7 and it gets killed.

So the second launch kills it by explosion at position 7.

So yes, we can "chain" the killing: push a monster to a larger position, then kill it at that larger position.

The key is that we can solve it greedily:

1. Sort the monster positions
2. For each monster, determine if it can be killed in a single shot or needs multiple shots
3. But actually, we can kill multiple monsters in one shot by targeting positions that kill some directly and push others

However, the optimal strategy seems to be:
- We can kill monsters at positions ≤ r by launching at positions ≥ their position (they get pushed to position -r ≤ 0 and die)
- For monsters at positions > r, we need to either kill them directly or push them further right and later kill them at their new position

But pushing them further right makes them bigger and harder to kill.

So for each monster at position x > r, we have two options:
- Kill directly at x: 1 shot
- Push it to x + r and kill at x + r: 2 shots

So we should prefer direct killing when possible.

But we might be able to kill multiple monsters in one shot by positioning the missile appropriately.

So the process seems to be:
1. We can choose any position c to launch
2. This kills all monsters at c directly
3. This pushes monsters to the left (y < c) to y - r
4. These pushed monsters might be killed by traps if y - r ≤ 0
5. This pushes monsters to the right (y > c) to y + r
6. Then we repeat

The important observation is that when we push a monster to y - r, it gets killed by trap if y - r ≤ 0, i.e., y ≤ r.

So any monster initially at position y ≤ r can be killed in the first shot by launching at c ≥ y.

For monsters at y > r, when they are pushed left (y < c), they move to y - r > 0 (since y > r), so they survive and get pushed further.

When we launch at c, monsters at y < c get pushed to y - r, and if y ≤ r, they are killed by trap.

So for any monster at position y ≤ r, we can kill it by launching at any position c ≥ y.

For a monster at position y > r:
- If we launch at c = y, it gets killed immediately
- If we launch at c < y, it gets pushed to y + r > y > r, making it even farther
- If we launch at c > y, it gets pushed to y - r > 0 (since y > r), so it survives

So the only way to kill a monster at position y > r is to either:
1. Launch at exactly y (killed directly)
2. Push it to some position and later kill it when we launch at that new position

But pushing it to y + r (by launching at c < y) means we can kill it later at position y + r.

So we can think of each monster at position y > r as being eventually "responsible for" a killing at position y or y + r.

But since we can launch at any position, and each launch can kill multiple monsters directly, we want to minimize the number of launches.

So if our set S includes any position >= y, then we can kill it by a launch at that position.

But we might not need to launch at a position in S for it, if we launch at a position in S that is >= y.

Actually, from example [3,4] with r=2: both positions > r=2, but we can kill both in one shot by launching at 4.

In [4,5] with r=2: we cannot kill both in one shot.

So the only way to kill a monster at position x > c is to have it at c, or to push it to x + r and kill it later.

Therefore, for a monster at position x > r, if we launch at c < x, it is pushed to x + r, so we need to launch at x + r to kill it.

So in the sorted array, if we have a monster at position x > r, and we don't launch at x, we must eventually launch at x + r to kill it.

Also, for a monster at position y <= r, if we launch at c >= y, it is killed by trap.

Therefore, the solution should be to check if there exists a position c such that for every monster at position x, either x = c or x < c and x - r <= 0.

In other words, if there exists a position c such that every monster at position x < c satisfies x - r <= 0, i.e., x <= r.

So for a fixed c, if for every x in the array with x < c, we have x <= r, then launching at c will kill all monsters at position c (directly) and kill all monsters at position x < c by trap (since x - r <= 0).

For monsters at position x > c, they are pushed to x + r > c > r, so they survive.

To kill them, we need to later launch at x + r or at x.

So the only way to kill in one shot is if there is a position c such that for every x in the array with x < c, x <= r, and for every x in the array with x > c, we can kill them in a subsequent shot, but that doesn't help for one shot.

For one shot to kill everything, we need that after the shot, all monsters are either at c or at a position <=0.

After launch at c:
- Monsters at c: killed
- Monsters at x < c: moved to x - r, which is <=0 if x <= r
- Monsters at x > c: moved to x + r > c > r, so >0

So only the monsters with x > c are not killed, and they are at x + r >0.

Therefore, unless there are no monsters with x > c, we cannot kill them in one shot.

So to kill in one shot, we need that there are no monsters at x > c for any c.

That means that the maximum position must be <= c for some c, which means that the maximum position must be <= c, and for all x < c, x <= r.

In other words, if we choose c = max(positions), then for all x < c, we need x <= r.

So the condition for one shot is: max(positions) <= c and for all x in positions, if x < c, then x <= r.

Since c can be max(positions), we need that for all x in positions with x < max(positions), x <= r.

In other words, all positions that are less than the maximum must be <= r.

In the example [3,4] with r=2: max=4, positions less than 4: only 3, and 3 > r=2, so condition fails, so not one shot.

But when we launch at 4, monster at 3 is pushed to 3-2=1≤0, so killed by trap, so it should be killed.

3-2=1, and 1 >0, so it is not <=0.

1 >0, so it is not killed by trap.

The condition for being killed by trap is that the new position <=0.

So x - r <=0, i.e., x <= r.

In this case, x=3, r=2, 3>2, so 3-2=1>0, so not killed by trap.

Therefore, in [3,4] with r=2, when we launch at 4, the monster at 3 is pushed to 1>0, so it survives, and we need a second shot to kill it at position 1.

So in that case, we need two shots.

Therefore, in [3,4] with r=2, answer is 2, not 1.

I made a mistake in the earlier reasoning.

When we launch at 4, for a monster at 3<4, it is pushed to 3-2=1>0, so it is not in the left part, so it is not killed by trap.

Only when x - r <=0, i.e., x <= r, it is killed by trap.

For x=3, r=2, 3>2, so 3-2=1>0, so not killed.

Therefore, only if x <= r, when x < c, it is killed by trap.

So in [3,4] with r=2, when launch at 4, monster at 3 is pushed to 1>0, so not killed, and monster at 4 is killed, so we need to kill the monster at 1 later.

- when launch at 4:

  - monster at 4: killed by explosion

Otherwise, two shots.

In the examples given:
- Example 1: positions [1,3,5], r=2
  - try c=5: for x<5: x=1,3. 1<=2, 3>2, so 3>2, condition fails for x=3.
  - try c=3: for x<3: x=1, 1<=2, yes. But there is a monster at 5>3, which will be pushed to 5+2=7>0, so not killed.
  - so we need at least two shots.
- Example 2: positions [5,2,3,5], r=1
  - try c=5: for x<5: x=2,3. 2>1, 3>1, so condition fails.
  - try c=2: for x<2: x=5>2, not in x<2, and x=2 is at c, so only x<2: none, so condition vacuously true? but there are monsters at 5>2, pushed to 5+1=6>0, so not killed.
  - actually, for c=2, monsters at x>2: 5,3,5, pushed to 6,4,6 >0, not killed.
  - so we need at least two shots.

Therefore, the algorithm is:

For a given query, the minimum number of shots is:
- 1 if there exists a position c such that for every monster at position x with x < c, we have x <= r, and also there are no monsters at position x > c (i.e., the maximum position is <= c)
- otherwise, 2

But if there are no monsters at x > c, then after launch at c, all monsters are either at c or at x < c with x <= r (so killed by trap) or at x > c with x <= c, which doesn't exist.

So to have no monsters at x > c, we need c >= max(positions)

Also, for every x in positions with x < c, x <= r.

So we need c >= max(positions) and for every x in positions with x < max(positions), x <= r.

In other words, if the maximum position is M, and all positions < M are <= r, then we can do in one shot by launching at M.

Otherwise, we need two shots.

Let me verify with examples:

Example 1: positions [1,3,5], r=2, M=5
- positions <5: 1,3
- 1<=2, 3>2, so 3>2, condition fails, so answer 2. Correct.

Example 2: positions [5,2,3,5], r=1, M=5
- positions <5: 2,3
- 2>1, 3>1, so condition fails, answer 2. Correct.

Example: positions [3,4] with r=3, M=4
- positions <4: 3, 3<=3, so yes, answer 1. Correct.

Example: positions [3,4] with r=2, M=4
- positions <4: 3>2, so condition fails, answer 2. Correct.

Example: one monster at 3, r=2, M=3
- positions <3: none, so condition vacuously true, answer 1. Correct.

Example: one monster at 3, r=1, M=3
- positions <3: none, so condition true, answer 1. But when we launch at 3, the monster at 3 is killed, so yes, answer 1. Correct.

Example: two monsters at 3, r=2, positions [3,3], M=3
- positions <3: none, condition true, answer 1. Correct.

Example: positions [1,3,4], r=2, M=4
- positions <4: 1,3; 1<=2, 3>2, so fails, answer 2.

When launch at 4: 
- monster at 1: 1<4, pushed to 1-2= -1 ≤0 → killed
- monster at 3: 3<4, pushed to 3-2=1>0 → not killed
- monster at 4: killed
- So need to kill the monster at 1 later, so at least two shots. Correct.

Therefore, the solution is:

For each query:
1. Sort the positions
2. Find M = max(positions)
3. Check if for every position x in the array with x < M, x <= r
4. If yes, output 1
5. Otherwise, output 2

Note: if there are no positions < M, then the condition is vacuously true, so output 1.

This matches the examples.

So the code is:

```python
q = int(input())
for _ in range(q):
    n, r = map(int, input().split())
    positions = list(map(int, input().split()))
    M = max(positions)
    # Check if all positions that are less than M are <= r
    # Since positions might have duplicates, and we only care about values < M
    # We can iterate through positions and if x < M and x > r, then condition fails
    found = False
    for x in positions:
        if x < M and x > r:
            found = True
            break
    if found:
        print(2)
    else:
        print(1)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Ivan plays an old action game called Heretic. He's stuck on one of the final levels of this game, so he needs some help with killing the monsters.

The main part of the level is a large corridor (so large and narrow that it can be represented as an infinite coordinate line). The corridor is divided into two parts; let's assume that the point $x = 0$ is where these parts meet.

The right part of the corridor is filled with $n$ monsters — for each monster, its initial coordinate $x_i$ is given (and since all monsters are in the right part, every $x_i$ is positive).

The left part of the corridor is filled with crusher traps. If some monster enters the left part of the corridor or the origin (so, its current coordinate becomes less than or equal to $0$), it gets instantly killed by a trap.

The main weapon Ivan uses to kill the monsters is the Phoenix Rod. It can launch a missile that explodes upon impact, obliterating every monster caught in the explosion and throwing all other monsters away from the epicenter. Formally, suppose that Ivan launches a missile so that it explodes in the point $c$. Then every monster is either killed by explosion or pushed away. Let some monster's current coordinate be $y$, then:

  if $c = y$, then the monster is killed;  if $y < c$, then the monster is pushed $r$ units to the left, so its current coordinate becomes $y - r$;  if $y > c$, then the monster is pushed $r$ units to the right, so its current coordinate becomes $y + r$. 

Ivan is going to kill the monsters as follows: choose some integer point $d$ and launch a missile into that point, then wait until it explodes and all the monsters which are pushed to the left part of the corridor are killed by crusher traps, then, if at least one monster is still alive, choose another integer point (probably the one that was already used) and launch a missile there, and so on.

What is the minimum number of missiles Ivan has to launch in order to kill all of the monsters? You may assume that every time Ivan fires the Phoenix Rod, he chooses the impact point optimally.

You have to answer $q$ independent queries.


-----Input-----

The first line contains one integer $q$ ($1 \le q \le 10^5$) — the number of queries.

The first line of each query contains two integers $n$ and $r$ ($1 \le n, r \le 10^5$) — the number of enemies and the distance that the enemies are thrown away from the epicenter of the explosion.

The second line of each query contains $n$ integers $x_i$ ($1 \le x_i \le 10^5$) — the initial positions of the monsters.

It is guaranteed that sum of all $n$ over all queries does not exceed $10^5$.


-----Output-----

For each query print one integer — the minimum number of shots from the Phoenix Rod required to kill all monsters.


-----Example-----
Input
2
3 2
1 3 5
4 1
5 2 3 5

Output
2
2



-----Note-----

In the first test case, Ivan acts as follows:   choose the point $3$, the first monster dies from a crusher trap at the point $-1$, the second monster dies from the explosion, the third monster is pushed to the point $7$;  choose the point $7$, the third monster dies from the explosion. 

In the second test case, Ivan acts as follows:   choose the point $5$, the first and fourth monsters die from the explosion, the second monster is pushed to the point $1$, the third monster is pushed to the point $2$;  choose the point $2$, the first monster dies from a crusher trap at the point $0$, the second monster dies from the explosion.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have monsters on a coordinate line with positive x-coordinates (right part)
2. If a monster's position becomes ≤ 0, it gets killed by crusher traps
3. When a missile explodes at point `c`:
   - Monsters at exactly position `c` are killed by explosion
   - Monsters to the left of `c` (y < c) are pushed left by `r` units (to y - r)
   - Monsters to the right of `c` (y > c) are pushed right by `r` units (to y + r)
4. We need to find the minimum number of missile shots to kill all monsters

## Key Insights

1. When we choose a missile impact point `c`, monsters to the left of `c` get pushed left, which might send them to ≤ 0 (killed by traps)
2. Monsters to the right get pushed right, making them farther away
3. Monsters exactly at `c` are killed instantly
4. The optimal strategy is to choose impact points that kill as many monsters as possible (especially those that will be pushed to ≤ 0)

## Strategy Analysis

Let me think about what happens when we shoot at a point:
- If we shoot at a point `c`, and there's a monster at position `x` where `x < c`, after pushing it left by `r`, it moves to `x - r`
- This monster gets killed if `x - r ≤ 0`, i.e., `x ≤ r`
- So any monster at position ≤ `r` that we shoot at a point ≥ `x` will be pushed to ≤ 0 and killed by traps

So algorithm:

For a query:
1. Let r be given, positions be given
2. Let max_pos = max(positions)
3. Group positions into:

4. If A is not empty, let c = max(A)  [we shoot at c]
5. After this shot:
   - For each position in A: 
        if position < c: pushed to position - r
        if position == c: killed
        if position > c: pushed to position + r  - but position > c and in A means c < position ≤ 2r, so position > c, pushed to position + r > c

Monsters with initial position x > r can only be killed by explosion if we shoot at exactly their current position.

For a monster at position x > 2r, after being pushed to x - r > r, it remains > r and needs to be killed by explosion at position x - r.

To minimize the additional shots, we want to choose c to maximize the number of monsters that are pushed to ≤ r or to positions that can be handled.

But in the optimal strategy, we can choose c to be a value that is ≥ the maximum of positions in A (positions ≤ r) and also ≥ some values to push some monsters to ≤ r.

For a monster at x > 2r, if we shoot at c such that c ≥ x, then it gets pushed to x + r > x > 2r, so to a larger position, which is bad.

If we shoot at c such that c < x, then it gets pushed to x - r > r (since x > 2r), so still > r.

- If c >= x: pushed to x + r > 2r + r = 3r > r
- If c < x: pushed to x - r > r

So it's always at > r after any shot.

The only way to reduce the number of such monsters is to have them pushed to a position that is then at > r, and then we shoot at that position.

So each such monster will eventually need to be shot at exactly its current position at some point.

Therefore, the additional shots needed is the number of distinct positions that the monsters at x > 2r end up at after being pushed in some shot.

To minimize this, we should choose the shot positions optimally.

We can choose a shot at a point c. This will push:
- Every monster at position x with x < c to x - r
- Every monster at position x with x > c to x + r

For a monster at x > 2r, if we can choose c such that x > c, it goes to x + r > 2r + r = 3r
If we choose c such that x < c, it goes to x - r > r

So in both cases, it goes to a position > r.

The only way to reduce the number of distinct positions is to have multiple monsters at the same final position.

For example, in example 2: we have two monsters at x>2: 3 and 5.
- If we shoot at c=3: 
  - Monster at 3: killed by explosion
  - Monster at 5: 5>3, pushed to 5+1=6

  - So we have one monster at 2 > r=1

In example 1: only one monster at x>4: 5
- Shoot at c=3: 5>3, pushed to 5+2=7
- Then shoot at 7: one additional shot
- Total: 1 + 1 = 2

So the number of additional shots is the number of distinct positions that the monsters from x>2r end up at after the first shot.

But how to compute that with one shot?

In the first shot, we can choose c to minimize the number of distinct final positions of the monsters from x>2r.

Each monster at x > 2r will go to:
- x - r if x < c
- x + r if x > c

So if we choose c, then the final positions are:
- For each x in positions with x > 2r:
  - if x < c: x - r
  - if x > c: x + r

We want to minimize the number of distinct values in this set.

Note that x - r and x + r are different for each x.

The optimal c might be chosen to group some of these values.

For example, if we have two monsters at x1 and x2 > 2r, we want to choose c such that either:
- x1 - r = x2 - r (impossible since x1 != x2)
- x1 - r = x2 + r (which would require x1 - x2 = 2r)
- or x1 - r = some value, x2 + r = same value

In general, for a set of values, we want to minimize the number of distinct values of (x - r) for x < c and (x + r) for x > c.

This is a classic problem in range partitioning.

We can try c = x for each x in positions > 2r, and see what happens.

But the number of such positions might be up to 10^5, and we have up to 10^5 queries, so we can't try all.

Alternatively, the minimum number of distinct final positions might be 1 if we can make all the final positions the same.

That would require for some value v, we have:
- for some x: x - r = v (so x = v + r)
- for some y: y + r = v (so y = v - r)

So we need to have both v + r and v - r in the set of positions > 2r.

But v - r must be > 2r, so v > 3r.

For a given v > 3r, if there exists x = v + r and y = v - r in the positions, then we can choose c such that v - r < c < v + r, then:
- y = v - r < c, so goes to (v - r) - r = v - 2r
- x = v + r > c, so goes to (v + r) + r = v + 2r
- not what we want.

I think I'm making a mess.

Let me go back to the known examples.

Example 1: positions [1,3,5], r=2
- Only one monster at > 4: 5
- After any shot, if we shoot at c<5, it goes to 5 - r = 3
- if c>5, goes to 5+r=7
- so distinct final positions: either 3 or 7, so 1 distinct value
- shots = 1 + 1 = 2

Example 2: positions [5,2,3,5], r=1
- positions > 2: 3,5
- if shoot at c=3: 
  - 3 at c: killed
  - 5>3: pushed to 5+1=6
  - final positions: {6} — distinct: 1
- if shoot at c=5:
  - 3<5: pushed to 3-1=2
  - 5 at c: killed
  - final positions: {2} — distinct: 1
- so in both cases, distinct = 1
- shots = 1 + 1 = 2

So in both examples, after the first shot, we have only one distinct final position from the >2r group.

Therefore, the number of additional shots is 1 if there is at least one such monster, or 0 if none.

But is that always true?

Counterexample: positions [6,7], r=2
- 2r=4
- positions > 4: 6,7
- shoot at c=5:
  - 6>5: pushed to 6+2=8
  - 7>5: pushed to 7+2=9
  - final positions: {8,9} — distinct: 2
- shoot at c=6:
  - 6 at c: killed
  - 7>6: pushed to 7+2=9
  - final positions: {9} — distinct: 1
- shoot at c=7:
  - 6<7: pushed to 6-2=4 > r=2 (4>2) — at 4
  - 7 at c: killed
  - final positions: {4} — distinct: 1

So we can choose c to make distinct final positions = 1.

Can we ever have more than 1 distinct final positions? Only if for any c, the set { x - r for x in positions > 2r and x < c } union { x + r for x in positions > 2r and x > c } has more than one distinct value.

But we can always choose c to be a value that is not in the set, and try to group.

In particular, if we have two monsters at x1 and x2 > 2r, we can always choose c such that x1 - r = x2 + r, if x1 - x2 = 2r.

Otherwise, we might not be able to group.

For example, positions [6,8], r=2
- x1=6, x2=8
- 6-2=4, 8+2=10, 4≠10
- can we find c such that 6-c and 8-c give the same final position?
- if c<6, then final positions: 6-r=4, 8-r=6
- if c>8, then final positions: 6+r=8, 8+r=10
- if 6<c<8, then final positions: 6-r=4, 8+r=10
- always at least two distinct values: 4,6 or 4,10 or 8,10

So distinct values will be at least 2.

Can we choose c to make them the same? Only if 6-r = 8+r, i.e., 6-8 = 2r, -2 = 2r, r = -1, impossible.

So in this case, for positions [6,8], r=2, after any shot, we will have at least two distinct final positions from the >2r group.

Then we need 2 additional shots.

But is that minimum? 

After first shot, we have monsters at positions 4 and 6 (if c<6) or at 8 and 10 (if c>8) or at 4 and 10 (if 6<c<8).

We then need to shoot at each distinct final position.

So minimum additional shots = number of distinct final positions after the first shot.

And since we can choose c, we want to minimize that number.

In this case, for [6,8], r=2, the values are:
- c<6: final positions: 4,6
- 6<c<8: final positions: 4,10
- c>8: final positions: 8,10
- So minimum distinct is 2 (e.g., in c<6: 4 and 6)

So additional shots = 2.

Total shots = 1 (first) + 2 = 3.

Is there a way to do it in 2 shots?

After shot 1 at c=6:
- monster at 6: killed
- monster at 8: 8>6, pushed to 8+2=10
- now only monster at 10
- shot 2 at 10: killed
- total 2 shots? Only 2 shots.

But positions: [6,8], r=2
- after shot at 6:
  - at 6: killed
  - at 8: moved to 8+2=10
- then shot at 10: killed at 10
- only 2 shots.

So only 2 shots, not 3.

What happened? I see the mistake.

After shooting at c=6:
- monster at 6: at c=6, so killed by explosion
- monster at 8: 8>6, so pushed to 8+2=10
- so only one new monster at 10
- then one shot at 10 to kill it
- total 2 shots.

So additional shots = 1.

The final position is 10, only one distinct.

So in this case, with c=6, we have only one distinct final position.

Why? Because the monster at 6 is at c=6, so it is killed by explosion, not pushed.

So for a monster at x > 2r, if x = c, it is killed by explosion, so only the monsters with x > c are pushed to x + r, and with x < c are pushed to x - r.

In the case of x = c, it is removed.

So in the set of positions > 2r, we have to consider that when x = c, it is killed, not pushed.

Therefore, after a shot at c, the final positions from >2r are:
- for each x > 2r and x < c: x - r
- for each x > 2r and x > c: x + r
- for each x > 2r and x = c: removed

So in the example [6,8], r=2, if we choose c=6:
- x=6: at c, killed
- x=8: 8>6, pushed to 8+2=10
- so only one final position: 10

If we choose c=7:
- x=6: 6<7, pushed to 6-2=4
- x=8: 8>7, pushed to 8+2=10
- final positions: 4,10 — distinct: 2

So minimum distinct is 1.

Therefore, the number of distinct final positions from >2r after the first shot is minimized, and we can always achieve at most 1 if there is one monster at >2r, or if we can group them.

In general, with one shot, we can reduce the number of monsters at >2r to one distinct final position if possible.

But can we always achieve 1 distinct final position?

No, for example: positions [6,7,9], r=2
- 6,7,9 > 4
- if we choose c=7:
  - 6<7: pushed to 4
  - 7 at c: killed
  - 9>7: pushed to 11
  - final positions: 4,11 — distinct: 2
- if c=6:
  - 6 at c: killed
  - 7>6: pushed to 9
  - 9>6: pushed to 11
  - final: 9,11 — distinct: 2
- if c=8:
  - 6<8: pushed to 4
  - 7<8: pushed to 5
  - 9>8: pushed to 11
  - final: 4,5,11 — distinct: 3
- if c=9:
  - 6<9: pushed to 4
  - 7<9: pushed to 5
  - 9 at c: killed
  - final: 4,5 — distinct: 2

So minimum is 2.

So we need 2 additional shots.

Therefore, the number of additional shots is the minimum over c of the number of distinct values in the set { x - r for x in positions > 2r and x < c } union { x + r for x in positions > 2r and x > c }.

This is what we need to compute.

But with up to 10^5 positions, and up to 10^5 queries, and total sum of n is 10^5, we can't try all c.

However, the only c that might be optimal are the positions in the set or between them.

In fact, since the function is piecewise constant, we can try only at the positions in the multiset of positions > 2r, and at the boundaries.

But the number of such positions might be large.

Since the total sum of n over all queries is at most 10^5, and each query has at most 10^5 positions, but the sum of n is 10^5, then the number of queries is at most 10^5, and the total number of positions is 10^5, so we can afford to try a limited number of c.

But in worst case, for one query, n=10^5, and we might have many positions > 2r, and we might need to try O(n) values of c, which would be O(n^2) = 10^10, too slow.

for _ in range(q):
    n, r = map(int, input().split())

    # Count distinct values > r
    distinct = set()
    for pos in x:
        if pos > r:
            distinct.add(pos)

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

After battling Shikamaru, Tayuya decided that her flute is too predictable, and replaced it with a guitar. The guitar has 6 strings and an infinite number of frets numbered from 1. Fretting the fret number j on the i-th string produces the note a_{i} + j.

Tayuya wants to play a melody of n notes. Each note can be played on different string-fret combination. The easiness of performance depends on the difference between the maximal and the minimal indices of used frets. The less this difference is, the easier it is to perform the technique. Please determine the minimal possible difference.

For example, if a = [1, 1, 2, 2, 3, 3], and the sequence of notes is 4, 11, 11, 12, 12, 13, 13 (corresponding to the second example), we can play the first note on the first string, and all the other notes on the sixth string. Then the maximal fret will be 10, the minimal one will be 3, and the answer is 10 - 3 = 7, as shown on the picture.

<image>

Input

The first line contains 6 space-separated numbers a_{1}, a_{2}, ..., a_{6} (1 ≤ a_{i} ≤ 10^{9}) which describe the Tayuya's strings.

The second line contains the only integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 100 000) standing for the number of notes in the melody.

The third line consists of n integers b_{1}, b_{2}, ..., b_{n} (1 ≤ b_{i} ≤ 10^{9}), separated by space. They describe the notes to be played. It's guaranteed that b_i > a_j for all 1≤ i≤ n and 1≤ j≤ 6, in other words, you can play each note on any string.

Output

Print the minimal possible difference of the maximal and the minimal indices of used frets.

Examples

Input


1 4 100 10 30 5
6
101 104 105 110 130 200


Output


0


Input


1 1 2 2 3 3
7
13 4 11 12 11 13 12


Output


7

Note

In the first sample test it is optimal to play the first note on the first string, the second note on the second string, the third note on the sixth string, the fourth note on the fourth string, the fifth note on the fifth string, and the sixth note on the third string. In this case the 100-th fret is used each time, so the difference is 100 - 100 = 0.

<image>

In the second test it's optimal, for example, to play the second note on the first string, and all the other notes on the sixth string. Then the maximal fret will be 10, the minimal one will be 3, and the answer is 10 - 3 = 7.

<image>

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Now solve the problem by providing the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a guitar with 6 strings, where string i produces note `a[i] + j` when fret j is used
2. We need to play n notes in a melody
3. For each note `b[k]`, we can choose which string to play it on, which determines the fret needed: `fret = b[k] - a[i]`
4. We want to minimize the difference between the maximum and minimum fret indices used across all notes

## Key Insights

1. For each note `b[k]`, if we play it on string i, the required fret is `b[k] - a[i]`
2. We need to assign each note to one of the 6 strings to minimize the range (max fret - min fret) of all frets used
3. Since we want to minimize the range, we should try to use frets that are as close to each other as possible
4. For a given assignment, the frets used are `{b[k] - a[i_k]}` for each note k, where i_k is the string chosen for note k
5. The range is `max(frets) - min(frets)`

## Strategy

This is a minimization problem where we need to assign notes to strings. The key insight is that we can use a greedy or optimization approach:

1. For each note, we can compute the fret value for each of the 6 strings
2. We want to find an assignment that minimizes the difference between the maximum and minimum frets
3. This can be solved by considering all possible ranges and checking if they're achievable

However, a more efficient approach is to realize that for any valid assignment, the minimum fret will be at least `min(b[k] - a[i])` over all notes and strings, and the maximum fret will be at most `max(b[k] - a[i])` over all notes and strings.

But here's a better insight: we can fix the minimum fret and see if we can achieve all notes with frets in that range, or fix the maximum fret and check if all notes can be covered.

Actually, let me think of a simpler approach:

Since we want to minimize `max(frets) - min(frets)`, we can try to find the optimal assignment by considering that each note must be assigned to some string, and the fret for that note is `b[k] - a[i]`.

We can use binary search on the answer (the range) and for each candidate range, check if it's possible to assign notes such that all frets fall within that range.

But there's an even simpler observation: for any note `b[k]`, the frets available are `b[k] - a[i]` for i in 1..6. We want to minimize the spread of these assigned frets.

Actually, since we can choose which string to use for each note, for each note we have a set of possible frets. The problem reduces to selecting one fret from each note's available frets such that the difference between the maximum and minimum selected frets is minimized.

This is similar to a "minimum range" selection problem. We can solve it by considering all possible pairs of extreme fret values and checking if a valid assignment exists.

But here's a more efficient approach: we can use the fact that the optimal solution will have the minimum fret equal to some value `x` and maximum fret equal to `x + d` for some `d`. We want to minimize `d`.

For a fixed range `[L, L+d]`, we can check if there's an assignment where all notes have frets in this range. This means for each note `b[k]`, we need to find a string `i` such that `L ≤ b[k] - a[i] ≤ L + d`, which is equivalent to `b[k] - (L + d) ≤ a[i] ≤ b[k] - L`.

So for each note, we need at least one string `i` where `a[i]` is in `[b[k] - (L + d), b[k] - L]`.

To find the minimum `d`, we can binary search on `d` from 0 to some upper bound, and for each `d`, check if there exists an `L` such that all notes can be assigned.

But this might be complex. Let me think of a simpler solution.

Actually, since the constraints are reasonable, we can try a different approach: we can consider all possible minimum fret values and for each, find the minimum maximum fret value needed, then take the difference.

But there's an even simpler insight: the optimal solution will have the minimum fret equal to some `f_min` and maximum fret equal to `f_max`, and for each note `b[k]`, we must have `f_min ≤ b[k] - a[i] ≤ f_max` for some string i, meaning `b[k] - f_max ≤ a[i] ≤ b[k] - f_min`.

For a fixed range `[f_min, f_max]`, we can check if all notes can be satisfied by verifying that for each note `b[k]`, there exists at least one string `i` such that `a[i]` is in `[b[k] - f_max, b[k] - f_min]`.

We can binary search on `f_max - f_min` (the range), and for each candidate range, check if there exists an assignment.

But the values of `f_min` can be very large (up to 10^9), so we need to be careful about the search space.

Actually, since we're only interested in the difference, we can binary search on the difference `d = f_max - f_min`, and for each `d`, check if there exists any `f_min` such that all notes can be assigned with frets in `[f_min, f_min + d]`.

The range of possible `f_min` values is bounded by the minimum and maximum of `(b[k] - a[i])` for all notes and strings.

So the algorithm:

1. Precompute all possible fret values: for each note `b[k]` and each string `i`, compute `fret = b[k] - a[i]`
2. Binary search on the range `d` (from 0 to some upper bound)
3. For a given `d`, check if there exists a `f_min` such that for every note `b[k]`, there exists a string `i` with `a[i]` in `[b[k] - (f_min + d), b[k] - f_min]`
4. This is equivalent to: for every note `b[k]`, the interval `[b[k] - (f_min + d), b[k] - f_min]` must contain at least one `a[i]`

We can rewrite this: for a fixed `d`, we want to know if there's an `f_min` such that for every note `b[k]`, the interval `[b[k] - (f_min + d), b[k] - f_min]` contains at least one `a[i]`.

This is equivalent to: for every note `b[k]`, the interval `[b[k] - (f_min + d), b[k] - f_min]` overlaps with at least one string value `a[i]`.

Let me define `f_min` as our candidate. For each note `k`, we need that `∃ i` such that `a[i] ∈ [b[k] - (f_min + d), b[k] - f_min]`.

This is equivalent to: `∃ i` such that `b[k] - (f_min + d) ≤ a[i] ≤ b[k] - f_min`.

Rearranging: `b[k] - a[i] ≤ f_min + d` and `b[k] - a[i] ≥ f_min`.

So for each note `k`, we need that there exists `i` such that `f_min ≤ b[k] - a[i] ≤ f_min + d`.

This means that the set of possible frets `{b[k] - a[i]}` for all strings and notes must have a subset that spans at most `d`.

Actually, a simpler approach: the minimum possible range is achieved when we select one fret value from each note's available frets such that the difference between the maximum and minimum selected values is minimized.

We can do this by considering all possible combinations, but that would be too slow.

Another idea: since we have only 6 strings, for each note we have only 6 choices. The complete set of possible fret assignments is small enough that we can use dynamic programming or greedy.

Wait, actually we can do this: the key insight is that the optimal solution will have the minimum fret equal to the minimum of some `b[k] - a[i]` and the maximum fret equal to the maximum of some `b[k] - a[i]`.

We can try to find the minimum possible range by considering all possible pairs of minimum and maximum frets that can be achieved through some assignment.

But with 100,000 notes, that's too many.

Let me go back to the binary search approach on the range `d`.

For a given `d`, we want to check if there exists an `f_min` such that for every note `k`, there exists a string `i` such that `f_min ≤ b[k] - a[i] ≤ f_min + d`.

This is equivalent to: for every note `k`, there exists a string `i` such that `b[k] - a[i] ∈ [f_min, f_min + d]`.

For a fixed `d`, we can check if there's an `f_min` that works by finding the "interval" of possible `f_min`.

For each note `k`, the condition requires that `f_min ≤ b[k] - a[i] ≤ f_min + d` for some string `i`.

Let `S_k = {b[k] - a[i] | i = 1..6}` be the set of possible frets for note k.

Then we need that `S_k ∩ [f_min, f_min + d]` is non-empty for every k.

This means that the interval `[f_min, f_min + d]` must intersect with every `S_k`.

So we want to find if there exists `f_min` such that `[f_min, f_min + d]` intersects with every `S_k`.

This is equivalent to: the union of all "forbidden" gaps has no gap larger than `d`.

A standard way to check this is to find the intersection of all possible intervals `[min(S_k), max(S_k)]`, but that's not right.

Actually, for each `S_k`, define `I_k` as the interval of `f_min` values such that `[f_min, f_min + d]` intersects `S_k`.

For a set `S_k`, the values of `f_min` such that `[f_min, f_min + d]` intersects `S_k` are those such that `f_min ≤ max(S_k)` and `f_min + d ≥ min(S_k)`, so `f_min ≥ min(S_k) - d`.

Thus, `f_min ∈ [min(S_k) - d, max(S_k)]`.

So for the system to have a solution, we need `f_min ∈ ∩_{k=1}^n [min(S_k) - d, max(S_k)]`, and this intersection must be non-empty.

Therefore, the condition is that `max(min(S_k) - d) ≤ min(max(S_k))`, which simplifies to `max(min(S_k)) - min(max(S_k)) ≤ d`.

Wait, that's not right. The intersection is `[max_k(min(S_k) - d), min_k(max(S_k))]`, and we need `max_k(min(S_k) - d) ≤ min_k(max(S_k))`.

This is equivalent to `max_k(min(S_k)) - d ≤ min_k(max(S_k))`, which means `max_k(min(S_k)) - min_k(max(S_k)) ≤ d`.

Therefore, a fixed `d` is feasible if and only if `d ≥ max_k(min(S_k)) - min_k(max(S_k))`.

But wait, this seems too simple. Let me verify with an example.

In the first example: a = [1,4,100,10,30,5], b = [101,104,105,110,130,200]

For note 101: S_1 = {100,97,1,91,81,96} → min=1, max=100

For note 200: S_6 = {200-1=199,200-4=196,200-100=100,200-10=190,200-30=170,200-5=195} → min=100, max=199

Now max(min(S_k)) = max(1,4,5,10,30,100) = 100
min(max(S_k)) = min(100,100,104,109,129,199) = 100

So max(min) - min(max) = 100 - 100 = 0

So d ≥ 0, and since d can be 0, the answer is 0.

Let me check the second example: a = [1,1,2,2,3,3], b = [13,4,11,12,11,13,12]

For note 13: S = {12,12,11,11,10,10} → min=10, max=12
For note 4: S = {3,3,2,2,1,1} → min=1, max=3  
For note 11: S = {9,9,8,8,7,7} → min=7, max=9

So max(min) - min(max) = 10 - 3 = 7

Which matches the expected output!

This seems to work! The minimal possible range is `max_k(min(S_k)) - min_k(max(S_k))`.

Wait, but is this always true? Let me think.

For each note k, we have a set S_k of possible frets. We need to choose one fret from each S_k such that the difference between the max and min of the chosen frets is minimized.

The key insight from the analysis above is that a range d is feasible if and only if `d >= max_k(min(S_k)) - min_k(max(S_k))`.

And when d equals this value, we can achieve it. 

Therefore, the minimal possible difference is `max_k(min(S_k)) - min_k(max(S_k))`.

Let me verify the intuition: the value `max_k(min(S_k))` represents the highest of the lower bounds of possible frets for each note, and `min_k(max(S_k))` represents the lowest of the upper bounds of possible frets for each note. The difference between these gives the minimum range that can accommodate all constraints.

Actually, let me reframe: for a fixed d, we need `[f_min, f_min + d]` to intersect with every S_k. This is possible if and only if the gap between the highest lower bound and the lowest upper bound is at most d.

So yes, the minimum d is `max_k(min(S_k)) - min_k(max(S_k))`.

Therefore, the algorithm is:

1. Read the strings a and the notes b
2. For each note b[k], compute the set of possible frets: S_k = {b[k] - a[i] for i in range(6)}
3. Find min_k(max(S_k)) and max_k(min(S_k))
4. Answer = max_k(min(S_k)) - min_k(max(S_k))

```python
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
n = int(input())
b = list(map(int, input().split()))

# For each note, find min and max possible frets
min_frets_for_notes = []
max_frets_for_notes = []

for note in b:
    frets = []
    for string_val in a:
        fret = note - string_val
        frets.append(fret)
    min_frets_for_notes.append(min(frets))
    max_frets_for_notes.append(max(frets))

# Find the maximum of minimum frets and minimum of maximum frets
max_min_fret = max(min_frets_for_notes)
min_max_fret = min(max_frets_for_notes)

result = max_min_fret - min_max_fret
print(result)
```

View Extracted Code 
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
n = int(input())
b = list(map(int, input().split()))

# For each note, find min and max possible frets
min_frets_for_notes = []
max_frets_for_notes = []

for note in b:
    frets = []
    for string_val in a:
        fret = note - string_val
        frets.append(fret)
    min_frets_for_notes.append(min(frets))
    max_frets_for_notes.append(max(frets))

# Find the maximum of minimum frets and minimum of maximum frets
max_min_fret = max(min_frets_for_notes)
min_max_fret = min(max_frets_for_notes)

result = max_min_fret - min_max_fret
print(result)

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

After battling Shikamaru, Tayuya decided that her flute is too predictable, and replaced it with a guitar. The guitar has 6 strings and an infinite number of frets numbered from 1. Fretting the fret number j on the i-th string produces the note a_{i} + j.

Tayuya wants to play a melody of n notes. Each note can be played on different string-fret combination. The easiness of performance depends on the difference between the maximal and the minimal indices of used frets. The less this difference is, the easier it is to perform the technique. Please determine the minimal possible difference.

For example, if a = [1, 1, 2, 2, 3, 3], and the sequence of notes is 4, 11, 11, 12, 12, 13, 13 (corresponding to the second example), we can play the first note on the first string, and all the other notes on the sixth string. Then the maximal fret will be 10, the minimal one will be 3, and the answer is 10 - 3 = 7, as shown on the picture.

<image>

Input

The first line contains 6 space-separated numbers a_{1}, a_{2}, ..., a_{6} (1 ≤ a_{i} ≤ 10^{9}) which describe the Tayuya's strings.

The second line contains the only integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 100 000) standing for the number of notes in the melody.

The third line consists of n integers b_{1}, b_{2}, ..., b_{n} (1 ≤ b_{i} ≤ 10^{9}), separated by space. They describe the notes to be played. It's guaranteed that b_i > a_j for all 1≤ i≤ n and 1≤ j≤ 6, in other words, you can play each note on any string.

Output

Print the minimal possible difference of the maximal and the minimal indices of used frets.

Examples

Input


1 4 100 10 30 5
6
101 104 105 110 130 200


Output


0


Input


1 1 2 2 3 3
7
13 4 11 12 11 13 12


Output


7

Note

In the first sample test it is optimal to play the first note on the first string, the second note on the second string, the third note on the sixth string, the fourth note on the fourth string, the fifth note on the fifth string, and the sixth note on the third string. In this case the 100-th fret is used each time, so the difference is 100 - 100 = 0.

<image>

In the second test it's optimal, for example, to play the second note on the first string, and all the other notes on the sixth string. Then the maximal fret will be 10, the minimal one will be 3, and the answer is 10 - 3 = 7.

<image>

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have 6 strings, each with a base note value `a[i]`
2. Fretting fret `j` on string `i` produces note `a[i] + j`
3. We need to play `n` notes from the melody
4. Each note can be played on any string-fret combination
5. We want to minimize the difference between the maximum and minimum fret indices used

## Key Insights

1. For a given note `b[k]`, if we play it on string `i`, the required fret is `b[k] - a[i]`
2. To minimize the difference between max and min frets, we want to use frets that are as close as possible to each other
3. For each note, we have multiple options (one for each string) to achieve it
4. The problem becomes: choose one fret value for each note such that the difference between the maximum and minimum fret values across all notes is minimized

## Approach

This is essentially a "range minimization" problem. We need to find the smallest possible range of fret values that can cover all notes.

Since we have multiple options for each note, we can think of this as:
- For each note, we can choose any of the 6 possible fret values (b[k] - a[i] for each string i)
- We want to select one fret value for each note such that the difference between the largest and smallest selected frets is minimized

A key observation: if we fix a fret value `x`, we can play a note `b[k]` on string `i` if and only if `b[k] - a[i] = x`, which means `b[k] = a[i] + x`.

But actually, we can achieve any fret value that is `b[k] - a[i]` for some string i and note k.

**Better approach**: For each note, we have a set of possible fret values. The optimal solution will have all selected fret values within some small range. We can use binary search on the answer (the difference between max and min frets).

For a given target range length `d`, can we achieve a solution where all frets are within a range of length `d`?

But there's an even simpler insight: since we can choose any of the 6 fret options for each note, we can think of it as we have a set of possible fret values for each note, and we want to find the minimum "spread" of these values.

Actually, let's think differently: for each note, we have possible fret values. The optimal solution will be to select one value from each note's possible values such that the range (max - min) is minimized.

This is similar to a "minimum range covering" problem. We can try all possible combinations of min fret values, and for each possible min fret value, we can determine what the max fret value needs to be, and minimize the range.

But with n up to 100,000, we can't try all combinations.

**Insight**: The optimal minimum fret value for each note will be one of the possible fret values for that note. And the optimal maximum fret value will be one of the possible fret values for the notes.

Since we're minimizing the range, we can consider all possible pairs of minimum and maximum fret values that could appear in an optimal solution.

However, there's a more elegant approach:

For each note `k`, the possible fret values are `{b[k] - a[i] for i in range(6)}`.

We can sort all possible fret values across all notes. Then, for any contiguous subsequence of these values, if we can assign each note to a fret value within this range, then the answer is at most the range of this subsequence.

But we need to ensure that for each note, we have at least one fret value within the selected range.

Actually, the answer is simply the minimum over all possible ranges of the maximum fret value minus minimum fret value, where the range covers at least one valid fret value for each note.

So here's the algorithm:
1. For each note, compute all possible fret values
2. Create a sorted list of all possible fret values (with positions indicating which note they belong to)
3. For each possible minimum fret value (from our list of fret values), use binary search to find the smallest maximum fret value such that every note has at least one fret value in [min_fret, max_fret]
4. Return the minimum difference

- We want to find the minimum range [L, R] that contains at least one fret value from each note's set

Let me implement the solution with the following steps:

1. Read inputs
2. For each note, compute all possible fret values (b[i] - a[j] for all j in range(6))
3. Create a list of all possible fret values, each with its note index
4. Sort this list by fret value
5. For each possible left value in this sorted list:
   a. For each note, find the minimum fret value >= left that is achievable
   b. The required right value is the maximum of these minimum values across all notes
   c. The range is right - left

But step 5a needs to be efficient. We can precompute for each note the sorted list of possible fret values.

Let me implement this.

```python
import bisect

# Read input
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
n = int(input())
b = list(map(int, input().split()))

# For each note, store the possible fret values
note_frets = []
for i in range(n):
    possible_frets = []
    for string_val in a:
        fret = b[i] - string_val
        possible_frets.append(fret)
    note_frets.append(possible_frets)

# Sort each note's fret values
for i in range(n):
    note_frets[i].sort()

# Create list of all fret values with their note index
all_frets = []
for i in range(n):
    for fret_val in note_frets[i]:
        all_frets.append(fret_val)

all_frets.sort()

min_diff = float('inf')

# For each possible left value (each unique fret value in our data)
for left in all_frets:
    # Find the minimum required right value
    max_required = 0
    for i in range(n):
        # Find the smallest fret value in note i that is >= left
        pos = bisect.bisect_left(note_frets[i], left)
        if pos < len(note_frets[i]):
            min_fret_for_note = note_frets[i][pos]
        else:
            # This shouldn't happen because we know b[i] > a[j] for all j
            min_fret_for_note = float('inf')
        max_required = max(max_required, min_fret_for_note)
    
    # The range is max_required - left
    diff = max_required - left
    min_diff = min(min_diff, diff)

print(min_diff)
```

```python
import bisect

# Read input
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
n = int(input())
b = list(map(int, input().split()))

# For each note, store the possible fret values
note_frets = []
for i in range(n):
    possible_frets = []
    for string_val in a:
        fret = b[i] - string_val
        possible_frets.append(fret)
    note_frets.append(possible_frets)

# Sort each note's fret values
for i in range(n):
    note_frets[i].sort()

# Create list of all unique fret values that appear in any note
all_frets = set()
for i in range(n):
    for fret_val in note_frets[i]:
        all_frets.add(fret_val)

# Convert to sorted list
all_frets = sorted(all_frets)

min_diff = float('inf')

# For each possible left value in all_frets
for left in all_frets:
    # For each note, find the smallest fret value >= left
    max_required = 0
    for i in range(n):
        # Find the first element >= left
        pos = bisect.bisect_left(note_frets[i], left)
        if pos < len(note_frets[i]):
            min_fret_for_note = note_frets[i][pos]
        else:
            # This should not happen since b[i] > a[j] for all j
            min_fret_for_note = float('inf')
        max_required = max(max_required, min_fret_for_note)
    
    # The range is max_required - left
    diff = max_required - left
    min_diff = min(min_diff, diff)

print(min_diff)
```

```python
import bisect

# Read input
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
n = int(input())
b = list(map(int, input().split()))

# For each note, store the possible fret values
note_frets = []
for i in range(n):
    possible_frets = []
    for string_val in a:
        fret = b[i] - string_val
        possible_frets.append(fret)
    note_frets.append(possible_frets)

# Sort each note's fret values
for i in range(n):
    note_frets[i].sort()

# Create list of all unique fret values that appear in any note
all_frets = set()
for i in range(n):
    for fret_val in note_frets[i]:
        all_frets.add(fret_val)

# Convert to sorted list
all_frets = sorted(all_frets)

min_diff = float('inf')

# For each possible left value in all_frets
for left in all_frets:
    max_required = 0
    valid = True
    for i in range(n):
        pos = bisect.bisect_left(note_frets[i], left)
        if pos >= len(note_frets[i]):
            valid = False
            break
        min_fret_for_note = note_frets[i][pos]
        max_required = max(max_required, min_fret_for_note)
    
    if valid:
        diff = max_required - left
        min_diff = min(min_diff, diff)

print(min_diff)
```

import bisect

# Read input
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
n = int(input())
b = list(map(int, input().split()))

# For each note, store the possible fret values
note_frets = []
for i in range(n):
    possible_frets = []
    for string_val in a:
        fret = b[i] - string_val
        possible_frets.append(fret)
    note_frets.append(possible_frets)

# Sort each note's fret values
for i in range(n):
    note_frets[i].sort()

# Create list of all unique fret values that appear in any note
all_frets = set()
for i in range(n):
    for fret_val in note_frets[i]:
        all_frets.add(fret_val)

# Convert to sorted list
all_frets = sorted(all_frets)

min_diff = float('inf')

# For each possible left value in all_frets
for left in all_frets:
    max_required = 0
    valid = True
    for i in range(n):
        pos = bisect.bisect_left(note_frets[i], left)
        if pos >= len(note_frets[i]):
            valid = False
            break
        min_fret_for_note = note_frets[i][pos]
        max_required = max(max_required, min_fret_for_note)
    
    if valid:
        diff = max_required - left
        min_diff = min(min_diff, diff)

print(min_diff)

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

After battling Shikamaru, Tayuya decided that her flute is too predictable, and replaced it with a guitar. The guitar has 6 strings and an infinite number of frets numbered from 1. Fretting the fret number j on the i-th string produces the note a_{i} + j.

Tayuya wants to play a melody of n notes. Each note can be played on different string-fret combination. The easiness of performance depends on the difference between the maximal and the minimal indices of used frets. The less this difference is, the easier it is to perform the technique. Please determine the minimal possible difference.

For example, if a = [1, 1, 2, 2, 3, 3], and the sequence of notes is 4, 11, 11, 12, 12, 13, 13 (corresponding to the second example), we can play the first note on the first string, and all the other notes on the sixth string. Then the maximal fret will be 10, the minimal one will be 3, and the answer is 10 - 3 = 7, as shown on the picture.

<image>

Input

The first line contains 6 space-separated numbers a_{1}, a_{2}, ..., a_{6} (1 ≤ a_{i} ≤ 10^{9}) which describe the Tayuya's strings.

The second line contains the only integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 100 000) standing for the number of notes in the melody.

The third line consists of n integers b_{1}, b_{2}, ..., b_{n} (1 ≤ b_{i} ≤ 10^{9}), separated by space. They describe the notes to be played. It's guaranteed that b_i > a_j for all 1≤ i≤ n and 1≤ j≤ 6, in other words, you can play each note on any string.

Output

Print the minimal possible difference of the maximal and the minimal indices of used frets.

Examples

Input


1 4 100 10 30 5
6
101 104 105 110 130 200


Output


0


Input


1 1 2 2 3 3
7
13 4 11 12 11 13 12


Output


7

In the first sample test it is optimal to play the first note on the first string, the second note on the second string, the third note on the sixth string, the fourth note on the fourth string, the fifth note on the fifth string, and the sixth note on the third string. In this case the 100-th fret is used each time, so the difference is 100 - 100 = 0.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a guitar with 6 strings, each with a base note `a[i]`
2. If we fret fret number `j` on string `i`, we get note `a[i] + j`
3. We need to play `n` notes from a melody
4. For each note `b[k]`, we can choose any string `i` and find the required fret `j = b[k] - a[i]`
5. We want to minimize the difference between the maximum and minimum fret indices used across all notes

## Key Insights

1. For each note `b[k]`, if we choose string `i`, we need fret `fret = b[k] - a[i]`
2. We want to minimize the range (max fret - min fret) across all notes
3. Since we can choose which string to use for each note, we have flexibility
4. The optimal strategy is to try to keep all fret values as close as possible

## Strategy

This is essentially a range minimization problem. For each note, we have multiple possible fret values (one for each string). We need to select one fret value per note such that the range of selected fret values is minimized.

A good approach is to consider that for each note, we can choose the fret value from the 6 possible strings. We want to find the minimal possible range.

We can use a sliding window approach or binary search on the answer. However, since we have discrete choices for each note, a more efficient approach is:

1. For each note, compute all possible fret values (b[k] - a[i] for each string i)
2. Sort all these possible fret values
3. Use a two-pointer or sliding window approach to find the minimal range

But actually, we can think of it differently: for a given range [L, R], we can check if it's possible to assign each note to a string such that the fret value falls within [L, R].

Let me implement a binary search on the range:

- Binary search on the answer (range)
- For a given range `r`, check if there exists an interval [L, L+r] such that for each note `b[k]`, there exists a string `i` such that `b[k] - a[i]` is in [L, L+r]
